    Learning by Confusion Locates a Parametrization-Dependent Fisher Information Maximum

    Authors: George Issa, Richard Scalettar

    Abstract: …balance condition for the Bayes-optimal confusion peak and show that, for equilibrium systems with a single swept parameter, the peak closely follows the maximum of the corresponding Fisher information. This immediately gives its finite-size scaling and reveals an unexpected dependence on how the data are sampled: the same configurations arranged on grids un… ▽ More Learning by confusion (LBC) is a machine-learning method for locating phase transitions without assuming a known order parameter. Its characteristic accuracy curve contains a central maximum that is interpreted as the transition point, but what determines the location and finite-size scaling of this maximum has remained unclear. We derive an exact balance condition for the Bayes-optimal confusion peak and show that, for equilibrium systems with a single swept parameter, the peak closely follows the maximum of the corresponding Fisher information. This immediately gives its finite-size scaling and reveals an unexpected dependence on how the data are sampled: the same configurations arranged on grids uniform in $T$, $\ln T$, or $β=1/T$ can yield different finite-size pseudocritical trajectories. Tests on the Ising, Blume-Capel, Potts, and Baxter-Wu models confirm the prediction across continuous, tricritical, and first-order transitions. Our results identify the statistical quantity measured by LBC and show that finite-size machine-learning estimates can depend on the parametrization of the training data as well as on the underlying physics. △ Less

    Authors: Oren Pedatzur, Noah Schwartz, D. Scott Acton, Ben Buky

    Abstract: …for future segmented-aperture space telescopes, including the Habitable Worlds Observatory (HWO). DBFH uses a set of carefully selected pupil-plane amplitude masks and their corresponding focal-plane intensity measurements to construct an algebraically linear inverse problem, enabling computationally efficient and stable reconstruction of the complex pupil field. Here, we present the first experimental demonstration of DBFH using a spatial light modulator and an HWO EAC5-like segmented-aperture geometry. We reconstruct challenging pseudo-random wavefronts and compare DBFH with the established Focus-Diverse Phase Retrieval (FDPR) method. The two methods achieve comparable reconstruction precision, while exhibiting distinct strengths and limitations. We further demonstrate DBFH wavefront sensing in the presence of a binary source, a scenario that is not naturally accommodated by conventional FDPR. These results provide experimental proof of concept and establish technology readiness level 3 for DBFH as a wavefront-sensing method for space telescopes with segmented primary mirrors. △ Less

    From Belief Inference to Task Action: A Multi-Level Evaluation of Theory of Mind on Verifiable Tasks

    Authors: Zhenshuai Yin, Yongxin Guo, Mingda Zhang, Zhongze Luo, Haoyue Liu, Lianmin Zhang, Xiaoying Tang

Many theory-of-mind (ToM) benchmarks assess mental-state inference through story questions, but correct answers do not establish that models can use these judgments in subsequent tasks. We introduce a multi-level evaluation built on existing, verifiable tasks.     cs.CV cs.LG

    WAVE: Wavelet-Controlled Coarse-to-Fine Guidance for Depth Super-Resolution

    Authors: Tayyab Nasir, Daochang Liu, Ajmal Mian

    Abstract: …convolutional hierarchies, inheriting their fine-to-coarse bias. Thus, low-level spatial cues surface in early layers, leaving the deeper layers to suppress those that do not correspond to true depth boundaries, which risks artifacts and blurred edges. The same fine-to-coarse bias persists in semantics-based methods that consume low-level tokens early and gl… ▽ More Guided depth super-resolution (GDSR) typically extracts RGB guidance features through convolutional hierarchies, inheriting their fine-to-coarse bias. Thus, low-level spatial cues surface in early layers, leaving the deeper layers to suppress those that do not correspond to true depth boundaries, which risks artifacts and blurred edges. The same fine-to-coarse bias persists in semantics-based methods that consume low-level tokens early and global tokens late. We present WAVE, which introduces a multi-level discrete wavelet transform (ML-DWT) as an explicit and interpretable feature-control mechanism, enabling a coarse-to-fine reconstruction by consuming sub-bands and semantic tokens in reverse of their generation order. WAVE further exploits these sub-bands to treat high- and low-frequency content separately, isolating the misleading RGB color and texture cues before any feature is learned so that their admission can be controlled explicitly, offering an intuitive alternative to the suppression learned implicitly by an opaque network. WAVE separates structure and detail reconstruction into dedicated modules that: i) model interactions within and across wavelet sub-bands, depth features, and semantic priors, ii) apply semantic gating to the high-frequency bands, and iii) fuse modalities through an invertible coupling mechanism that prevents collapse onto a single modality. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that WAVE matches or outperforms existing methods, with the largest gains at high upsampling factors, where low-resolution depth contains the least structure. △ Less

    Exceptional times of compact support for $α$-stable superprocesses

    Authors: Jieliang Hong, Leonid Mytnik

    Abstract: …is contained in any prescribed neighborhood of the extinction point have Hausdorff dimension one in every nontrivial interval ending at extinction. These results extend the corresponding conclusions for $0<α<2/3$ to the full stable range. The key new ingredient is a uniform zero-set theorem of independent interest for Feller branching diffusions starte… ▽ More We consider symmetric $α$-stable Dawson--Watanabe superprocesses on $\mathbb R^d$ for every $d\geq1$ and $0<α<2$, started from an arbitrary nonzero finite measure. We prove that, almost surely, the times at which the spatial support is compact and nonempty have Hausdorff dimension one in every nontrivial interval before extinction. We also prove that, almost surely, the times at which the support is contained in any prescribed neighborhood of the extinction point have Hausdorff dimension one in every nontrivial interval ending at extinction. These results extend the corresponding conclusions for $0<α<2/3$ to the full stable range. The key new ingredient is a uniform zero-set theorem of independent interest for Feller branching diffusions started from zero with deterministic continuous cumulative immigration. It shows that sufficiently small total immigration ensures that the zero set on a prescribed open interval has Hausdorff dimension arbitrarily close to one, with arbitrarily high probability. The estimate is uniform over the immigration profile and allows singular continuous immigration measures. △ Less

    Linear Hashing Is Poissonesque

    Authors: Vinayak M. Kumar

    Abstract: …-linear map is no more than the corresponding expectation for a fully random map $\textit{plus}$ $\frac{3}2 + o(1)$. Specializing to $m\gg n\log n$, we show that the expected max load is $(1+o(1))m/n$ in this regime. 2. $\textbf{Concentration.}$ When $m\gg n\log (1/ρ)$ balls are tossed into $n$ bins randomly, we expect with $0.99$ probability that… ▽ More The Poisson approximation theorem states that when $m$ balls are tossed uniformly and randomly into $n$ bins, the distribution of the bin loads can sometimes be approximated by $n$ independent Poisson random variables of mean $m/n$. We show that when the balls are tossed via a random $\mathbb{F}_2$-linear map, the load distribution is still governed by this Poisson distribution, albeit in a (necessarily) weaker manner. This allows us to show that a random linear map behaves like a fully random map in the following ways: 1. $\textbf{Expected loads.}$ We show that the expected value of the $r$th largest bin is governed by the $r$th largest value among $n$ independent Poisson random variables of mean $m/n$. Specializing to the canonical setting of $m=n$ and $r=1$, our bounds show that the expected maximum load when $n$ balls are hashed into $n$ bins by a random $\mathbb{F}_2$-linear map is no more than the corresponding expectation for a fully random map $\textit{plus}$ $\frac{3}2 + o(1)$. Specializing to $m\gg n\log n$, we show that the expected max load is $(1+o(1))m/n$ in this regime. 2. $\textbf{Concentration.}$ When $m\gg n\log (1/ρ)$ balls are tossed into $n$ bins randomly, we expect with $0.99$ probability that $\textit{every}$ density-$ρ$ subset of bins will contain a $(1\pm o(1) )ρ$-fraction of the balls. We show that this remains the case if the $m$ balls are tossed using a random linear map, and our bound matches the $o(1)$-factor produced by the fully random case. This gives a stronger version of the Leftover Hash Lemma for linear maps. An implication of this is that all bins have load $m/n \pm O(\sqrt{(m/n)\log n})$ with probability $0.99$. We obtain these results by showing that the average of a log-potential on the bin loads is upper bounded by the cumulant generating function of a Poisson random variable of mean $m/n$. △ Less
